\eqref{eq:dp}, transposed to track minimum time for a target scaled
value rather than maximum value for a target time; the recursion is
}\[
\DIFdel{g(i,v) = \min\Bigl(g(i-1,v),\ \bigl[g(i-1,v-w_i') + p_i \le d_i\bigr]
\cdot \bigl(g(i-1,v-w_i') + p_i\bigr)\Bigr),
}\]%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\mathcal F_i(v)$ be the family of
feasible $S\subseteq\{1,\dots,i\}$ with $\sum_{j\in S}w_j'=v$ and
$g(i,v):=\min\{\sum_{j\in S}p_j:S\in\mathcal F_i(v)\}$
($\min\emptyset=+\infty$). Then $g(0,0)=0$, $g(0,v)=+\infty$ for $v>0$, and
for $i\ge1$
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:g}
g(i,v)=\begin{cases}
\min\{\,g(i-1,v),\ g(i-1,v-w_i')+p_i\,\} & \text{if } v\ge w_i' \text{ and } g(i-1,v-w_i')+p_i\le d_i,\\[2pt]
g(i-1,v) & \text{otherwise.}
\end{cases}
}\end{equation}\DIFaddend 
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{by the same argument as in Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}'s proof,
with the roles
of the time and value axes exchanged.
This table has $O(n \cdot
n^2/\epsilon) = O(n^3/\epsilon)$ entries, each computed }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{The sets of $\mathcal F_i(v)$ that do not contain $i$ are exactly
$\mathcal F_{i-1}(v)$. Let $S=S'\cup\{i\}$ with $i\notin S'$. Serving $S$
}\DIFaddend in \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$O(1)$ time, so the whole computation runs in time polynomial in $n$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{non-decreasing deadline order puts $i$ last, so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:edd} $S$ is feasible iff $S'$ is feasible and
$p(S')+p_i\le d_i$, where $p(S')=\sum_{j\in S'}p_j$. Thus the sets of
$\mathcal F_i(v)$ that contain $i$ are exactly $S'\cup\{i\}$ with
$S'\in\mathcal F_{i-1}(v-w_i')$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$1/\epsilon$.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$p(S')\le d_i-p_i$. The admissibility
condition is an upper bound on $p(S')$ and the objective $p(S')+p_i$ is
increasing in $p(S')$ (}\emph{\DIFadd{dominance}}\DIFadd{): if some admissible $S'$ exists
then a minimizer of $p$ over $\mathcal F_{i-1}(v-w_i')$ is admissible and
optimal, and if that minimizer is not admissible no $S'$ is. Hence the
minimum over sets containing $i$ is $g(i-1,v-w_i')+p_i$ if this is finite
and at most $d_i$ (and $v\ge w_i'$), and $+\infty$ otherwise. Taking the
minimum with the sets that avoid $i$ gives \eqref{eq:g}. If $w_i'=0$ the
first line returns $g(i-1,v)$, so zero-scaled sites are never selected.
}\end{proof}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \begin{theorem}[FPTAS]\label{thm:fptas}
\DIFaddend Let \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\hat S$ be the feasible subset of largest scaled value $v$ with $g(n,v) < \infty$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\epsilon\in(0,1)$, $w_{\max}=\max_iw_i$, $K=\max\{1,\epsilon
w_{\max}/n\}$, $w_i'=\lfloor w_i/K\rfloor$, and let $\hat S$ be a feasible
set maximizing $\sum_{i\in\hat S}w_i'$ (Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} returns
one). It runs in $O(n^3/\epsilon)$ time}\DIFaddend , and
\DIFaddbegin \begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{if $\epsilon w_{\max}\le n$ (so $K=1$), $W(\hat S)=W^\ast$;
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{if $\epsilon w_{\max}>n$ and $n\ge2$, put $y=n/\epsilon$,
$N=\lfloor y\rfloor$ and $\varphi=y-N$. Then
}\begin{gather*}
\DIFadd{W(\hat S)\ \ge\ \rho_{n,\epsilon}\,W^\ast,\qquad
\rho_{n,\epsilon}:=\Bigl(1+\max\Bigl\{\tfrac{n-1}{y},\ \tfrac{n-2+\varphi}{N}\Bigr\}\Bigr)^{-1},}\\
\DIFadd{\rho_{n,\epsilon}\ \ge\ \frac{N}{N+n-1}\ >\ \frac{1}{1+\epsilon}\ >\ 1-\epsilon .
}\end{gather*}
\end{enumerate}
\DIFadd{In particular $W(\hat S)>(1-\epsilon)W^\ast$, the classical guarantee,
and $W(\hat S)>W^\ast/(1+\epsilon)$; the constant $\rho_{n,\epsilon}$
cannot be improved (Proposition~\ref{prop:tight}). For $n=1$ the optimum
is trivial.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{\DIFadd{Running time.}} \DIFadd{In both cases $w_i'\le w_{\max}/K\le n/\epsilon$, so
$V=\sum_iw_i'\le n^2/\epsilon$ and the table has $O(n\cdot V)=O(n^3/\epsilon)$
cells, each filled in $O(1)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:g}.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Case (a).}} \DIFadd{Then $w_i'=w_i$, so $\hat S$ maximizes $W$ over feasible
sets, i.e.\ $W(\hat S)=W^\ast$.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Case (b).}} \DIFadd{Now $K=\epsilon w_{\max}/n>1$ exactly, so $w_{\max}=Ky$.
Let $r_i:=w_i-Kw_i'\in[0,K)$ and let $h$ be a site with $w_h=w_{\max}$;
then $w_h'=N\ge1$ and $r_h=K\varphi$. Put $x=W(\hat S)$ and
$\hat v=\sum_{i\in\hat S}w_i'$, }\DIFaddend let $S^\ast$ be \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the true optimal subset (value
$W^\ast = \sum_{i\in S^\ast} w_i$). We consider two cases, according to
which argument of $K=\max(1,\epsilon w_{\max}/n)$ attains the maximum.
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\emph{\DIFdel{Case $\epsilon w_{\max}/n < 1$.}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{an optimal set,
$A=S^\ast\setminus\hat S$ and $B=\hat S\setminus S^\ast$. Three facts:
}\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(F1)}] \DIFadd{$x\ge K\hat v$, since $w_i\ge Kw_i'$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(F2)}] \DIFadd{$\hat v\ge w_h'=N$, since $\{h\}$ is feasible
(Assumption~\ref{as:feasible}) and $\hat S$ maximizes the scaled value; in
particular $\hat S\ne\emptyset$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(F3)}] \DIFadd{$W^\ast-x\le\sum_{i\in A}r_i$. Indeed $\hat S$ maximizes the
scaled value, so $\sum_Bw_i'\ge\sum_Aw_i'$ after cancelling
$S^\ast\cap\hat S$, and $W^\ast-x=\sum_Aw_i-\sum_Bw_i\le\sum_Aw_i-K\sum_Bw_i'
\le\sum_A(w_i-Kw_i')$.
}\end{enumerate}
\DIFadd{Since $A\cap\hat S=\emptyset$ and $\hat S\ne\emptyset$, $|A|\le n-1$. Write
$R:=\sum_Ar_i$; if $A=\emptyset$ then $W^\ast\le x$ and there is nothing
to prove, so let $A\ne\emptyset$.
}\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{\DIFadd{$h\in\hat S$.}} \DIFaddend Then \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$K=1$, so $w_i' = \lfloor
w_i \rfloor = w_i$ for every $i$ (the $w_i$ are integers by
Definition~\ref{def:mwhed}) : the scaling is vacuous, the
dynamic
program above is exactly Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}'s dynamic program, and it
returns $\hat S = S^\ast$, so $W(\hat S) = W^\ast \ge (1-\epsilon)
W^\ast$ trivially (as $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ and $W^\ast \ge 0$).
}%DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$x\ge w_h=Ky$ and $R<K|A|\le K(n-1)$, so
$W^\ast/x<1+(n-1)/y$.
}\item \DIFaddend \emph{\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Case $\epsilon w_{\max}/n \ge 1$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$h\in A$}\DIFaddend .} Then \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$K = \epsilon w_{\max}/n$
exactly (not merely an upper bound), }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$R=r_h+\sum_{A\setminus h}r_i\le K(\varphi+n-2)$ and,
by (F1)--(F2), $x\ge KN$, so $W^\ast/x\le1+(n-2+\varphi)/N$.
}\item \emph{\DIFadd{$h\notin A\cup\hat S$.}} \DIFadd{Then $A,\hat S\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
\setminus\{h\}$ are disjoint with $\hat S\ne\emptyset$, }\DIFaddend so \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$Kn = \epsilon w_{\max}$
exactly.
Since $w_i' \le w_i/K$, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$|A|\le n-2$,
$R<K(n-2)$, $x\ge KN$, and $W^\ast/x<1+(n-2)/N$, which is dominated by
the previous case.
}\end{itemize}
\DIFadd{This proves $W(\hat S)\ge\rho_{n,\epsilon}W^\ast$. Since $\varphi<1$ }\DIFaddend we have
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$\sum_{i \in S^\ast} w_i' \ge
\sum_{i\in S^\ast}(w_i/K - 1) \ge W^\ast/K - n$, and $S^\ast$ is itself
a feasible subset achieving this scaled value or better, so the returned $\hat S$ satisfies $\sum_{i \in \hat S} w_i' \ge W^\ast/K - n$.
Converting back, since $w_i \le K(w_i' + 1)$, }\begin{align*}
\DIFdel{W(\hat S) = \sum_{i\in \hat S} w_i
}&\DIFdel{\ \ge\ K \sum_{i \in \hat S} w_i'
\ \ge\ K\Bigl(\frac{W^\ast}{K} - n\Bigr) = W^\ast - Kn }\\
&\DIFdel{\ =\ W^\ast - \epsilon\, w_{\max}
\ \ge\ W^\ast - \epsilon\, W^\ast = (1-\epsilon) W^\ast,
}\end{align*}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{using $Kn = \epsilon w_{\max}$ (this case) and $w_{\max} \le W^\ast$
(Assumption~\ref{as:feasible}). Either case gives $W(\hat S) \ge
(1-\epsilon)W^\ast$.}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\rho_{n,\epsilon}\ge N/(N+n-1)$, and $N>y-1=n/\epsilon-1\ge(n-1)/\epsilon$
(as $1/\epsilon>1$), i.e.\ $(n-1)/N<\epsilon$, so $N/(N+n-1)>1/(1+\epsilon)
>1-\epsilon$. (The classical bound also follows directly from (F3):
$W^\ast-x\le R<Kn=\epsilon w_{\max}\le\epsilon W^\ast$, using
$w_{\max}\le W^\ast$; Appendix~\ref{app:fptas-detail} spells this
derivation out.)
}\DIFaddend \end{proof}

\begin{example}[The FPTAS on the running instance]\label{ex:fptas}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas} to Example~\ref{ex:running} with a
deliberately loose $\epsilon = 0.5$. The scaling factor is $K =
\max(1,\, 0.5 \cdot 10/4) = 1.25$, and the scaled weights are $w' =
(\lfloor 5/1.25\rfloor, \lfloor 8/1.25\rfloor, \lfloor 3/1.25\rfloor,
\lfloor 10/1.25\rfloor) = (4,6,2,8)$. Re-running the dynamic program on
these scaled weights (tracking minimum dispatch time per scaled value,
as in the proof) again selects $S=\{1,2,4\}$, recovering the true
optimum $W^\ast=23$ exactly---comfortably inside the guaranteed bound
$(1-\epsilon)W^\ast = 11.5$. \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{This mirrors Section~\ref{sec:experiments}'s
finding at scale: the FPTAS's worst-case bound is rarely tight in
practice, even at a loose $\epsilon$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Here the
scaling is active ($K>1$) yet harmless; Section~\ref{sec:scaling} shows
that this is typical of random instances, while
Proposition~\ref{prop:tight} gives instances on which the guarantee of
Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas} is nearly attained}\DIFaddend .
\end{example}

Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} states the FPTAS's value-indexed construction
explicitly. It has exactly the same shape as Algorithm~\ref{alg:exact},
with the roles of the time axis and the (scaled) value axis exchanged,
which is why its table size no longer depends on $P$.

\begin{algorithm}
\caption{FPTAS for MWHED (Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas})}\label{alg:fptas}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{algorithmic}[1]
%DIFDELCMD < \Require sites $1,\dots,n$ with $d_1\le\dots\le d_n$, dispatch times $p_i$, weights $w_i$, accuracy $\epsilon\in(0,1)$
%DIFDELCMD < \Ensure a dispatch order with on-time weight $\ge (1-\epsilon)W^\ast$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $w_{\max} \gets \max_i w_i$; \ $K \gets \max(1,\, \epsilon\, w_{\max}/n)$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $w_i' \gets \lfloor w_i/K \rfloor$ for $i=1,\dots,n$; \ $V \gets \sum_{i=1}^n w_i'$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $g[0][0] \gets 0$; \ $g[0][v] \gets +\infty$ for $v=1,\dots,V$
%DIFDELCMD < \For{$i = 1,\dots,n$}
%DIFDELCMD <   \For{$v = 0,\dots,V$}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $g[i][v] \gets g[i-1][v]$ \Comment{exclude site $i$}
%DIFDELCMD <     \If{$v \ge w_i'$ \textbf{and} $g[i-1][v-w_i'] < +\infty$ \textbf{and} $g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i \le d_i$}
%DIFDELCMD <       \If{$g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i < g[i][v]$}
%DIFDELCMD <         \State $g[i][v] \gets g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i$
%DIFDELCMD <         \State $\mathit{choice}[i][v] \gets \textsc{true}$
%DIFDELCMD <       \EndIf
%DIFDELCMD <     \EndIf
%DIFDELCMD <   \EndFor
%DIFDELCMD < \EndFor
%DIFDELCMD < \State $v^\ast \gets \max\{v : g[n][v] < +\infty\}$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $\hat S \gets \emptyset$; \ $v \gets v^\ast$
%DIFDELCMD < \For{$i = n,\dots,1$} \Comment{backtrack, as in Algorithm~\ref{alg:exact}}
%DIFDELCMD <   \If{$\mathit{choice}[i][v]$}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $\hat S \gets \hat S \cup \{i\}$; \ $v \gets v - w_i'$
%DIFDELCMD <   \EndIf
%DIFDELCMD < \EndFor
%DIFDELCMD < \State \Return $\hat S$, dispatched in increasing-deadline order
%DIFDELCMD < \end{algorithmic}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require sites with dispatch times $p_i$, deadlines $d_i$, weights $w_i$, accuracy $\epsilon\in(0,1)$
\Ensure a dispatch order with on-time weight $\ge \rho_{n,\epsilon}W^\ast>(1-\epsilon)W^\ast$
\State delete every site with $p_i > d_i$; relabel so $d_1\le\dots\le d_n$
\State $w_{\max} \gets \max_i w_i$; \ $K \gets \max(1,\, \epsilon\, w_{\max}/n)$
\State $w_i' \gets \lfloor w_i/K \rfloor$ for $i=1,\dots,n$; \ $V \gets \sum_{i=1}^n w_i'$
\State $g[0][0] \gets 0$; \ $g[0][v] \gets +\infty$ for $v=1,\dots,V$
\For{$i = 1,\dots,n$}
  \For{$v = 0,\dots,V$}
    \State $g[i][v] \gets g[i-1][v]$ \Comment{exclude site $i$}
    \If{$v \ge w_i'$ \textbf{and} $g[i-1][v-w_i'] < +\infty$ \textbf{and} $g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i \le d_i$}
      \If{$g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i < g[i][v]$}
        \State $g[i][v] \gets g[i-1][v-w_i'] + p_i$
        \State $\mathit{choice}[i][v] \gets \textsc{true}$
      \EndIf
    \EndIf
  \EndFor
\EndFor
\State $v^\ast \gets \max\{v : g[n][v] < +\infty\}$
\State $\hat S \gets \emptyset$; \ $v \gets v^\ast$
\For{$i = n,\dots,1$} \Comment{backtrack, as in Algorithm~\ref{alg:exact}}
  \If{$\mathit{choice}[i][v]$}
    \State $\hat S \gets \hat S \cup \{i\}$; \ $v \gets v - w_i'$
  \EndIf
\EndFor
\State \Return $\hat S$, dispatched in increasing-deadline order
\end{algorithmic}
\DIFaddend \end{algorithm}

\DIFaddbegin \begin{remark}[Zero-scaled sites and an optional completion step]\label{rem:completion}
\DIFadd{When $K>1$ a site with $w_i<K$ has scaled weight $w_i'=0$ and, because
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} minimizes dispatch time for each scaled value,
such a site is never selected. The bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas}
accounts for the weight so lost (at most $K$ per site), and
Proposition~\ref{prop:tight} shows the loss can approach the fraction
$1-1/(1+\epsilon)$ of the optimum. A natural post-processing step, which cannot decrease the
returned weight and so preserves Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas}, is to
re-insert rejected sites in decreasing order of weight while the set
stays feasible (checkable in $O(n)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:edd}); we use it
as the ``completed'' variant in Section~\ref{sec:scaling}.
}\end{remark}

\DIFaddend \subsection{A second tractable special case\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: equal-size sites}\DIFaddend }\DIFaddbegin \label{sec:equalp}
\DIFaddend 

Theorem~\ref{thm:hardness} shows that heterogeneous dispatch costs,
weighed against heterogeneous criticality under a shared deadline,
already suffice for hardness. The next result shows \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that }\DIFaddend the reverse
axis---heterogeneous deadlines under a shared dispatch cost---does not.
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{We state it for $m\ge1$ vehicles that need not be identical. In
}\emph{\DIFadd{MWHED-$m$}} \DIFadd{each of $m$ vehicles, all starting at the depot at time
$0$, serves a sequence of round trips; each site is served by at most one
vehicle; vehicle $v$ is occupied for $p_v>0$ at every site it serves
(different $p_v$ model vehicles of different speeds over the same
distance); and a site is on time if its completion time on its vehicle is
at most $d_i$. Identical vehicles are the case $p_v=p$, and MWHED with
equal dispatch times is the case $m=1$. As the introduction and
Section~\ref{sec:related} note, this is the scheduling of unit-time jobs
on identical or uniform machines; we include a self-contained proof.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{theorem}[Equal dispatch cost is tractable]%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{For readers who do not work with matroids we recall the notions used.
A }\emph{\DIFadd{matroid}} \DIFadd{on a finite ground set $E$ is a family $\mathcal I$ of
}\emph{\DIFadd{independent}} \DIFadd{subsets of $E$ with (i) $\emptyset\in\mathcal I$;
(ii) if $B\in\mathcal I$ and $A\subseteq B$ then $A\in\mathcal I$; and
(iii) the }\emph{\DIFadd{augmentation property}}\DIFadd{: if $A,B\in\mathcal I$ and
$|A|<|B|$, then $A\cup\{x\}\in\mathcal I$ for some $x\in B\setminus A$.
The }\emph{\DIFadd{greedy algorithm}} \DIFadd{considers the elements of $E$ in order of
non-increasing weight and keeps an element if the set kept so far plus
that element is still independent. The }\emph{\DIFadd{transversal matroid}} \DIFadd{of a
bipartite graph has as independent sets the subsets of one side that can
be matched into the other side; it is used here with sites on one side
and (vehicle, position) }\emph{\DIFadd{slots}} \DIFadd{on the other. We prove the matroid
property we need directly rather than cite it. A }\emph{\DIFadd{union--find}}
\DIFadd{(disjoint-set) structure maintains a partition of $\{0,1,\dots,n\}$ under
}\textsc{\DIFadd{find}}\DIFadd{, which returns the representative of a block, and
}\textsc{\DIFadd{union}}\DIFadd{, which merges two blocks. With union by size (or rank) and
path compression, a sequence of $n$ operations costs $O(n\,\alpha(n))$
time \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{kortevygen2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, where $\alpha$ is the inverse Ackermann
function, which grows more slowly than any iterated logarithm; path
compression alone, with arbitrary linking, only gives $O(\log n)$
amortized time per operation, and for this interval structure linear time
is possible on a RAM \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{gabow1985}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\begin{theorem}[Equal-size sites are tractable for any number of vehicles]\DIFaddend \label{thm:equalp}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{If $p_i = p$ for all }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{Vehicle $v\in\{1,\dots,m\}$ needs time $p_v>0$ for every site, so that
the $j$-th site served by $v$ completes at time $jp_v$; site }\DIFaddend $i$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(all sites equidistant from the depot , in
round-trip dispatch time
), MWHED is solvable exactly in $O(n \log n)$
time .
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{has
deadline $d_i$ and weight $w_i>0$ and is on time iff it completes by
$d_i$. Put $C(t):=\sum_{v=1}^m\lfloor t/p_v\rfloor$, the number of pairs
$(v,j)$ with $j\ge1$ and $jp_v\le t$. Then
}\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{a set $S$ can be served entirely on time iff
$|\{i\in S:d_i\le t\}|\le C(t)$ for every $t\ge0$;
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{the feasible sets are the independent sets of a matroid;
}\item[\DIFadd{(c)}] \DIFadd{the greedy algorithm (non-increasing weight; keep a site iff
the kept set stays feasible) is optimal, and it can be implemented, with
an explicit schedule, in $O(m+n\log n)$ time.
}\end{enumerate}
\DIFadd{For identical vehicles, $p_v\equiv p$, we have $C(t)=m\lfloor t/p\rfloor$
and the running time is $O(n\log n)$ for every $m$ (and $m\le n$ may be
assumed).
}\DIFaddend \end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{With equal dispatch costs, the $k$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \emph{\DIFadd{Step 1: slots, no gaps.}} \DIFadd{Call $(v,j)$, $j\ge1$, a }\emph{\DIFadd{slot}} \DIFadd{with
time $\tau(v,j):=jp_v$. We claim that $S$ is feasible iff there is an
injective map $\pi$ from $S$ to slots with $\tau(\pi(i))\le d_i$ for all
$i\in S$. Given a schedule, let $\pi(i)$ be the slot site $i$ occupies.
Conversely, given $\pi$, serve on each vehicle its sites in increasing
order of the position of $\pi(i)$, now at positions $1,2,\dots$: the
$r$}\DIFaddend -th \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dispatched site (whichever it is )
completes at time $kp$, regardless of order. Assigning a site to
``dispatch position'' }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{site served by $v$ has position $r$ at most that of its slot, so it
completes no later than $\tau(\pi(i))\le d_i$.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Step 2: the counting criterion (a).}} \DIFadd{($\Rightarrow$) The sites of
$S$ with $d_i\le t$ need distinct slots of time at most $t$, and there
are $C(t)$ such slots. ($\Leftarrow$) List all slots by non-decreasing
time, $\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\dots$ (ties arbitrary), and let $s_1,\dots,s_k$
be the sites of $S$ by non-decreasing deadline. The sites $s_1,\dots,s_r$
all have deadline at most $d_{s_r}$, so $r\le C(d_{s_r})$: at least $r$
slots have time at most $d_{s_r}$, whence $\tau(\sigma_r)\le d_{s_r}$. The
map $s_r\mapsto\sigma_r$ satisfies Step~1. Since $k\le n$ it uses only
$\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n$: }\emph{\DIFadd{only the $n$ earliest slots matter}}\DIFadd{.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Step 3: matroid (b).}} \DIFadd{Properties (i) and (ii) are clear from (a).
Let $A,B$ be feasible with $|A|<|B|$, write $N_X(t):=|\{i\in X:d_i\le t\}|$
and let $0=\tau_0<\tau_1<\dots<\tau_q$ be $0$ and the distinct deadlines
of $A\cup B$. Let $a$ be the largest index with $N_B(\tau_a)\le
N_A(\tau_a)$; since $N_B(\tau_q)=|B|>|A|=N_A(\tau_q)$ we have $a<q$ and
$N_B(\tau_j)\ge N_A(\tau_j)+1$ for all $j>a$. Subtracting
$N_B(\tau_a)\le N_A(\tau_a)$ from the case $j=a+1$ shows that $B$ has more
sites with deadline exactly $\tau_{a+1}$ than $A$ does, so some
$x\in B\setminus A$ has $d_x=\tau_{a+1}$. Adding $x$ to $A$ leaves $N(t)$
unchanged for $t<\tau_{a+1}$ and raises it by one for $t\ge\tau_{a+1}$,
where $N_A(t)+1\le N_B(t)\le C(t)$ because $B$ is feasible. By (a),
$A\cup\{x\}$ is feasible.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Step 4: greedy is optimal.}} \DIFadd{Let $G=\{g_1,\dots,g_r\}$ be the set the
greedy algorithm returns (weights $w_{g_1}\ge\dots\ge w_{g_r}$, ties
broken by index), and let $O=\{o_1,\dots,o_s\}$ be any feasible set with
$w_{o_1}\ge\dots\ge w_{o_s}$. $G$ is maximal feasible, so augmentation
gives $s\le r$. We claim $w_{g_k}\ge w_{o_k}$ for all $k\le s$, which
proves $w(G)\ge w(O)$. Suppose $w_{g_k}<w_{o_k}$ for some }\DIFaddend $k$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is therefore feasible for that site if and only if $kp \le d_i$, i.
e.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Apply
augmentation to $A=\{g_1,\dots,g_{k-1}\}$ and $B=\{o_1,\dots,o_k\}$: some
$o_j\in B\setminus A$ makes $A\cup\{o_j\}$ feasible, and
$w_{o_j}\ge w_{o_k}>w_{g_k}$. The greedy algorithm examined $o_j$ before
$g_k$, when its kept set was a subset of $A$; by property (ii) that set
plus $o_j$ was feasible, so greedy kept $o_j$, i.e.\ $o_j\in G$ and, being
examined before $g_k$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$k \le \lfloor d_i/p \rfloor =: D_i$. The problem becomes: choose an
injective assignment of a subset of
sites to positions $1,\dots,n$, each site }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$o_j\in A$---a contradiction.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Step 5: implementation (c).}} \DIFadd{(i) Only the $\min\{m,n\}$ fastest
vehicles can contribute to $\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n$ (a vehicle appears
only if its first slot does); select them in $O(m)$ time by linear-time selection. (ii) Generate
$\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n$ with a heap holding the next slot time $jp_v$
of each vehicle, recording $(v,j)$ for every popped slot:
$O(n\log\min\{m,n\})$. (iii) For each site let $D_i:=|\{k\le n:\tau(\sigma_k)\le d_i\}|
=\min\{n,C(d_i)\}$, by binary search in $O(\log n)$; a site with $D_i=0$ is
discarded. Site }\DIFaddend $i$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{assigned to a position at
most $D_i$, maximizing the total weight of assigned sites . This is the classical problem of scheduling }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{can use exactly the slots $\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_{D_i}$,
so by Steps~1--2 a set is feasible iff its sites can be given distinct
indices $k_i\le D_i$, i.e.\ iff it is feasible for }\DIFaddend unit-time \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{jobs with deadlines to maximize total profit. The family of subsets that admit such a feasibleassignment forms a matroid (the transversal matroid of the bipartite
feasibility relation between sites and positions), and on any matroid,
the greedy algorithm---here: sort }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{jobs with
deadlines $D_i$ on one machine. (iv) Sort the }\DIFaddend sites by decreasing \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$w_i$, and assign
each in turn to the latest available position
$k \le D_i$, or leave it
unassigned if noneis available---returns a maximum-weight independent
set, i. e. , an optimal solution \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep[Ch.~13]{kortevygen2018}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. Sorting
takes $O(n \log n)$ time, and maintaining the set of available
positions under repeated ``find andremove the latest available
position $\le D_i$'' queries can be implemented with a }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{weight
and run Algorithm~\ref{alg:matroid}: maintain a }\DIFaddend union--find \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{structure in $O(n \alpha(n))$ additional time, giving $O(n \log n)$
overall.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{structure over
$\{0,\dots,n\}$ in which $\textsc{find}(q)$ is the largest free position
$\le q$ (or $0$ if none); to process site $i$ let $q:=\textsc{find}(D_i)$;
if $q\ge1$ keep $i$, assign it the slot $\sigma_q$ and merge $q$ into
$q-1$, otherwise discard $i$. To keep the $O(n\,\alpha(n))$ bound we link
by size and store at each tree root $r$ a }\emph{\DIFadd{label}} \DIFadd{$\ell(r)$, the
smallest position of its block, so that $\textsc{find}(q)$ returns
$\ell(\mathrm{root}(q))$. The rule keeps $i$ exactly when the kept set plus
$i$ is feasible. If $q\ge1$ the construction exhibits a feasible
assignment. If $q=0$, let $q^\ast$ be the largest $q\ge D_i$ such that
positions $1,\dots,q$ are all taken. Every kept site occupying a position
$\le q^\ast$ has $D\le q^\ast$: a kept site with $D>q^\ast$ would have
been given the latest free position $\le D$, and position $q^\ast+1$ was
free then (it is free now; if $q^\ast=n$ no kept site has $D>q^\ast$
since $D_i\le n$), so it would sit above $q^\ast$. Hence the kept set has
$q^\ast$ sites with $D\le q^\ast$ and, as $D_i\le q^\ast$, adding $i$
violates the criterion of Step~2. (v) Serve on each vehicle its assigned
sites in increasing slot position (Step~1). The cost is $O(m)$ for (i),
$O(n\log n)$ for (ii)--(iv) including the sort, and $O(n\alpha(n))$ for
the union--find operations: $O(m+n\log n)$ in total. (The $O(n\log n)$
bound also holds with path compression alone.) For identical vehicles the
slots are $\sigma_k=(k\bmod m,\lceil k/m\rceil)$, no heap is needed, and
the total is $O(n\log n)$.
}\DIFaddend \end{proof}

\DIFaddbegin \begin{remark}
\DIFadd{The proof uses only that $C$ is a non-decreasing integer-valued function
and that the slots can be listed in order of time; vehicles that become
available at times $a_v$ (slot times $a_v+jp_v$) are covered as well. Site
release dates are }\emph{\DIFadd{not}} \DIFadd{covered (Section~\ref{sec:disc-multi}), and
sites of unequal sizes are NP-hard already for $m=1$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:hardness}).
}\end{remark}

\DIFaddend \begin{example}[The matroid greedy on an equal-cost instance]\label{ex:matroid}
Consider four sites all at the same round-trip distance \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$p=2$ }\DIFdelend from the depot,
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{so that $p=2$ for identical vehicles, }\DIFaddend with deadlines $d=(3,3,5,9)$ and
weights $w=(9,4,7,6)$\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. With one vehicle ($m=1$) the earliest slots have
times $2,4,6,8$}\DIFaddend , so the latest feasible \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{dispatch position for }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{position of }\DIFaddend each site is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$D_i = \lfloor
d_i/p\rfloor = (1,1,2,4)$}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$D=(1,1,2,4)$}\DIFaddend .
Sorted by decreasing weight, the greedy order is site $1$ ($w=9$), site
$3$ ($w=7$), site $4$ ($w=6$), site $2$ ($w=4$). Site $1$ takes position
$1$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(its only option)}\DIFdelend ; site $3$ takes position $2$; site $4$ takes position $4$; site $2$
needs a position $\le 1$, but position $1$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{already }\DIFdelend taken, so \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{site $2$ }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{it }\DIFaddend is left
unassigned. The greedy solution dispatches sites $1,3,4$ for a total
weight of $9+7+6=22$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, sacrificing only the lowest-weight site whose
deadline conflicts with a higher-weight site's---exhaustive }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; exhaustive }\DIFaddend search over all $16$ subsets confirms
\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{that }\DIFaddend $22$ is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{indeed optimal. }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{optimal. With two identical vehicles ($m=2$) the earliest
slots have times $2,2,4,4$ and $D=(2,2,4,4)$: site $1$ takes position $2$,
site $3$ position $4$, site $4$ position $3$ and site $2$ position $1$, so
all four sites are served, for $26$ (vehicle~A serves sites $2$ and $4$,
completing at $2$ and $4$; vehicle~B serves sites $1$ and $3$, completing
at $2$ and $4$).
}\DIFaddend \end{example}

\begin{algorithm}
\caption{Matroid greedy for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{equal-cost MWHED }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{equal-size MWHED-$m$ }\DIFaddend (Theorem~\ref{thm:equalp})}\label{alg:matroid}
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{algorithmic}[1]
%DIFDELCMD < \Require sites $1,\dots,n$, common dispatch time $p$, deadlines $d_i$, weights $w_i$
%DIFDELCMD < \Ensure an optimal on-time subset $S^\ast$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $D_i \gets \lfloor d_i/p \rfloor$ for $i=1,\dots,n$ \Comment{latest feasible position for site $i$}
%DIFDELCMD < \State sort sites into order $\pi_1,\dots,\pi_n$ with $w_{\pi_1} \ge w_{\pi_2} \ge \dots \ge w_{\pi_n}$
%DIFDELCMD < \State $\mathit{available} \gets \{1,\dots,n\}$; \ $S^\ast \gets \emptyset$
%DIFDELCMD < \For{$k = 1,\dots,n$}
%DIFDELCMD <   \State $i \gets \pi_k$
%DIFDELCMD <   \If{$\exists$ position $q \in \mathit{available}$ with $q \le D_i$}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $q^\ast \gets \max\{q \in \mathit{available} : q \le D_i\}$ \Comment{latest such position}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $S^\ast \gets S^\ast \cup \{i\}$; \ $\mathit{available} \gets \mathit{available} \setminus \{q^\ast\}$
%DIFDELCMD <   \EndIf
%DIFDELCMD < \EndFor
%DIFDELCMD < \State \Return $S^\ast$, dispatched in increasing-deadline order
%DIFDELCMD < \end{algorithmic}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require sites with deadlines $d_i$ and weights $w_i$; vehicle dispatch times $p_1,\dots,p_m$ (all equal for identical vehicles)
\Ensure an optimal on-time subset $S^\ast$ and an explicit schedule
\State generate the $n$ earliest slots $\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n$, with times $\tau_1\le\dots\le\tau_n$, using a heap over the vehicles' next slot times
\State $D_i\gets|\{k:\tau_k\le d_i\}|$ (binary search); discard sites with $D_i=0$
\State $\textsc{find}$ over $\{0,\dots,n\}$: every $q$ its own block; \ $S^\ast\gets\emptyset$
\For{each site $i$ in order of non-increasing $w_i$}
  \State $q \gets \textsc{find}(D_i)$ \Comment{largest free position $\le D_i$, or $0$}
  \If{$q\ge1$}
    \State $S^\ast\gets S^\ast\cup\{i\}$; \ assign $i$ to slot $\sigma_q$; \ \textsc{union}$(q,q-1)$ \Comment{$q$ is now represented by $q-1$}
  \EndIf
\EndFor
\State \Return $S^\ast$, each vehicle serving its assigned sites in order of slot time
\end{algorithmic}
\DIFaddend \end{algorithm}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hardness-boundary}
Theorems~\ref{thm:hardness} and~\ref{thm:equalp} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{together }\DIFdelend show that neither
heterogeneous dispatch cost nor heterogeneous deadlines alone is
responsible for MWHED's hardness\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{: it is specifically the interaction
between heterogeneous dispatch cost }\emph{\DIFdel{and}} %DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{heterogeneous
criticality, competing under a shared deadline structure, that produces
a knapsack-hard problem}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{; Theorem~\ref{thm:classification}
below makes this exact}\DIFaddend . A dispatch network in which every site is
equally far from the depot---a plausible approximation for, e.g., a
ring of substations around a central facility, or short-radius urban
service territories---remains exactly solvable regardless of how
heterogeneous the sites' hazard deadlines or criticalities are\DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{, and
regardless of the number of vehicles, identical or not}\DIFaddend .
\end{remark}

\subsection{The cost of not \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{optimizing}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{reconsidering}\DIFaddend }

Theorems~\ref{thm:dp} and~\ref{thm:fptas} give planners exact and
near-exact tools, but a planner \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{who instead falls back on the simplest conceivable heuristic---dispatch }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{might instead fall back on a simple
rule. The simplest is to dispatch }\DIFaddend sites in increasing-deadline order and
simply accept whichever happen to still be on time, never skipping a
site to make room for a later, more valuable \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{one---receives no
guaranteeat all}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{one (}\emph{\DIFadd{naive EDD}}\DIFadd{; a
site that is already late still consumes its dispatch time). A slightly
smarter rule skips a site that would arrive late (}\emph{\DIFadd{EDD with
skipping}}\DIFadd{). Neither has any guarantee}\DIFaddend .

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{proposition}[Naive earliest-deadline dispatch has unbounded worst-case ratio]%%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{proposition}[Deadline-order dispatch has unbounded worst-case ratio]\DIFaddend \label{prop:naive}
For every $r \in (0,1)$ there is an MWHED instance, satisfying
Assumption~\ref{as:feasible}, on which \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the naive policy's }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{both naive EDD and EDD with
skipping obtain }\DIFaddend on-time weight \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{is }\DIFdelend strictly less than $r \cdot W^\ast$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix $r \in (0,1)$ and choose an integer $W > 1/r$. Consider the
two-site instance with $p = (1, 2)$, $d = (1, 2)$, $w = (1, W)$; both
sites satisfy Assumption~\ref{as:feasible} ($p_i = d_i$). Since
$d_1 < d_2$, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the naive policy dispatches }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{both rules dispatch }\DIFaddend site $1$ first, completing at time
$1 \le d_1$ (on time, weight $1$)\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, then site }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{. Site }\DIFaddend $2$ \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{, completing }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{would then complete }\DIFaddend at time
$1+2=3 > d_2=2$\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{(exposed, losing weight $W$); its }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{: naive EDD serves it anyway and it is exposed, and EDD
with skipping discards it; either way the }\DIFaddend total is $1$. Dispatching site
$2$ alone \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{instead }\DIFdelend completes it at time $2 \le d_2$, \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{on time, }\DIFdelend for weight $W$; \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{this is feasible and,
}\DIFdelend since \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$W > 1 \ge$
the value of any subset not containing site $2$, it is }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$W>1$
this is }\DIFaddend optimal, so \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$W^\ast = W$.
The naive policy's ratio is therefore }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$W^\ast = W$. The ratio is }\DIFaddend $1/W < r$.
\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < 

%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \end{proof}

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{Proposition~\ref{prop:naive} is not merely a boundary case: the two
sites here differ only
by a factor of two in dispatch time,
yet the naive policy's loss can be made arbitrarily large by scaling $W$ alone. No fixed constant-factor guarantee is
possible for a policy that never
reconsiders an early,
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{The loss comes from never giving up an early, }\DIFaddend low-value \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{commitment against alater, }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{commitment for a
later, }\DIFaddend high-value \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{one---exactly the reconsideration that Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}'s dynamic
program and Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas}'s
FPTAS perform, and that Example~\ref{ex:running}
already previewed at a modest scale. Section~\ref{sec:experiments}
confirms the same qualitative gap persists, if less extremely, on
random instances of realistic size}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{one; skipping doomed sites removes only the waste of
serving a site that is already late. It is natural to ask whether a
local repair rule avoids the problem. The weighted generalization of
Moore--Hodgson's rule used as a heuristic in
Section~\ref{sec:experiments}---process sites in deadline order and,
whenever the running schedule becomes infeasible, discard the scheduled
site of smallest weight-to-time ratio---does very well on random
instances, but it too has no guarantee.
}

\begin{algorithm}
\caption{\DIFadd{Weighted greedy repair (heuristic; no guarantee)}}\label{alg:repair}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require sites with $d_1\le\dots\le d_n$ (after deleting sites with $p_i>d_i$), dispatch times $p_i$, weights $w_i$
\Ensure a feasible on-time subset $\mathit{kept}$ (not necessarily optimal)
\State $\mathit{kept} \gets \emptyset$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets 0$
\For{$i = 1,\dots,n$}
  \State $\mathit{kept} \gets \mathit{kept} \cup \{i\}$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets \mathit{total} + p_i$
  \While{$\mathit{total} > d_i$ \textbf{and} $\mathit{kept} \ne \emptyset$}
    \State $j \gets \arg\min_{j' \in \mathit{kept}} w_{j'}/p_{j'}$ \Comment{smallest weight-to-time ratio}
    \State $\mathit{kept} \gets \mathit{kept} \setminus \{j\}$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets \mathit{total} - p_j$
  \EndWhile
\EndFor
\State \Return $\mathit{kept}$, dispatched in increasing-deadline order
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\DIFadd{Its output is always feasible, by the same inductive argument as for the
unweighted algorithm: removing a site can only shorten the completion
time of every site that remains.
}

\begin{proposition}[Weighted greedy repair has unbounded worst-case ratio]\label{prop:repair}
\DIFadd{For every integer $k\ge2$ the two-site instance $p=(1,k)$, $d=(1,k)$,
$w=(2,2k-1)$ satisfies Assumption~\ref{as:feasible} and the weighted
greedy repair of Algorithm~\ref{alg:repair} returns on-time weight $2$,
while $W^\ast=2k-1$; the ratio $2/(2k-1)\to0$}\DIFaddend .
\DIFaddbegin \end{proposition}
\DIFaddend 

%DIF <  ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\DIFdelbegin \section{\DIFdel{Numerical illustration}}%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{section}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{sec:experiments}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{proof}
\DIFadd{The algorithm keeps site $1$ (total $1\le d_1$). On adding site $2$ the
total becomes $1+k>d_2=k$, so it discards the kept site of smallest
ratio $w_j/p_j$: site $1$ has ratio $2$ and site $2$ has ratio
$(2k-1)/k<2$, so site $2$ is discarded and the result is $\{1\}$ with
weight $2$. Site $2$ alone completes at $k\le d_2$, so $W^\ast\ge2k-1$,
and the two sites cannot both be on time, so $W^\ast=2k-1$ for $k\ge2$.
}\end{proof}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{We illustrate Theorems~\ref{thm:dp} and~\ref{thm:fptas} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:naive} in five complementary experiments: an
accuracy comparison across four algorithms on synthetic instances; a
runtime comparison isolating the pseudo-polynomial (}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{These two propositions are elementary two-site examples and we offer
them as illustrations, not as deep results; on random instances the
greedy repair is far better than its worst case
(Section~\ref{sec:acc}). They do say that some form of reconsideration
governed by the weights }\emph{\DIFadd{and}} \DIFadd{the dispatch times is needed for any
worst-case guarantee. They do not say that the dynamic program or the
FPTAS is the only way to obtain one: other polynomial algorithms with
guarantees may exist, and we do not claim otherwise.
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Further structural results}}\label{sec:structure}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Tightness of the FPTAS guarantee.}} \DIFaddend Theorem~\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{\ref{thm:dp}) versus truly polynomial (Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas}) dependence on the instance's numerical magnitude;
a sensitivity sweep over the FPTAS's
accuracy parameter $\epsilon$; a robustness check repeating the accuracy comparison under two alternative synthetic instance-generation
regimes; }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{\ref{thm:fptas}
guarantees the constant $\rho_{n,\epsilon}$, which exceeds both the
classical $1-\epsilon$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$1/(1+\epsilon)$. The next result shows that
it cannot be improved for Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} as stated.
}

\begin{proposition}[The refined guarantee is asymptotically tight]\label{prop:tight}
\DIFadd{Let $\epsilon\in(0,1)$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{finally, a small instance built directly from public
records of a real wildfire (Section~\ref{sec:campfire}), to confirm
the synthetic findings are not an artifact of how the synthetic
instances are generated.
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$n\ge2$, and $y=n/\epsilon$, $N=\lfloor y\rfloor$,
$\varphi=y-N$. For every $\eta>0$ there is an instance with $n$ sites satisfying
Assumption~\ref{as:feasible} on which Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} returns a
set of weight at most $(\rho_{n,\epsilon}+\eta)W^\ast$. Hence the constant
$\rho_{n,\epsilon}$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas} is best possible, and it
tends to $1/(1+\epsilon)$ as $n\to\infty$; the classical guarantee
$1-\epsilon$ is not attained (it is loose by the factor
$1/(1-\epsilon^2)$ in the limit).
}\end{proposition}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin \subsection{\DIFdel{Accuracy across instance sizes}}
%DIFAUXCMD
\addtocounter{subsection}{-1}%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{proof}
\DIFadd{Let $M$ be a large integer and $K=\epsilon M/n$ (so $K\to\infty$ with
$M$). }\emph{\DIFadd{Family~1}} \DIFadd{(the term $\tfrac{n-1}{y}$): $p_i=1$, $d_i=n$ for
all $i$, $w_1=M$ and $w_i=\lceil K\rceil-1$ for $i\ge2$. All sites fit, so
$W^\ast=M+(n-1)(\lceil K\rceil-1)$; site $1$ has scaled weight $N$ and all
the others scaled weight $0$, so the algorithm returns $\{1\}$ (zero-scaled
sites are never selected) and the ratio
$M/W^\ast\to(1+(n-1)\epsilon/n)^{-1}=(1+(n-1)/y)^{-1}$.
}\emph{\DIFadd{Family~2}} \DIFadd{(the term $\tfrac{n-2+\varphi}{N}$): a site $b$ with
$(p,d,w)=(1,1,\lceil KN\rceil)$, a site $h$ with $(2,2,M)$, and $n-2$
sites $z_j$ with $(1,n,\lceil K\rceil-1)$. Then $w_{\max}=M=Ky$, so
$w_b'=w_h'=N$ and $w_{z}'=0$; $b$ and $h$ are incompatible ($1+2>2$), so
the maximal scaled value is $N$ and the minimal time $1$ is attained only
by $\{b\}$: the algorithm returns $\{b\}$, of weight $\lceil KN\rceil$,
whereas $\{h\}\cup\{z_j\}$ is feasible ($h$ completes at $2$, the $z_j$ at
$3,\dots,n$), so $W^\ast\ge M+(n-2)(\lceil K\rceil-1)$. The ratio is at
most $\lceil KN\rceil/(M+(n-2)(\lceil K\rceil-1))\to
N/(y+n-2)=(1+(n-2+\varphi)/N)^{-1}$. The smaller of the two limits is
$\rho_{n,\epsilon}$.
}\end{proof}

\DIFadd{The constant concerns Algorithm~\ref{alg:fptas} }\emph{\DIFadd{without}} \DIFadd{the
completion step; with the step the guarantee can only improve, and we do
not determine its exact value. Rescaling is also available: running the
scheme with $\epsilon'=\delta/(1-\delta)$ guarantees $1-\delta$ for
$\delta<\tfrac12$, so the refinement changes constants, not the
dependence on $1/\epsilon$.
}

\DIFadd{Family~1 is the extremal one whenever $N(1-\varphi)\ge\varphi(n-2+\varphi)$, in particular
when $\epsilon$ divides $n$; Family~2 is needed otherwise (for $n=3$,
$\epsilon=10/13$ the two limits are $0.661$ and $0.612$). The optional
completion step of Remark~\ref{rem:completion} repairs Family~1
completely, which is why we report both variants in
Section~\ref{sec:scaling}.
}\DIFaddend 

\DIFaddbegin \paragraph{\DIFadd{Which heterogeneity causes hardness?}} \DIFadd{Theorems~\ref{thm:hardness}
and~\ref{thm:equalp} and Moore's algorithm together settle the
complexity of every restriction obtained by making some of the three
data vectors constant. }\DIFaddend For \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$n \in \{10,15,20,25,30,50,75,100\}$ we generate 20 random
instances each }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$T\subseteq\{p,w,d\}$ let $\mathcal C_T$ be
the class of MWHED instances in which each vector named in $T$ is
constant ($p_i=p$, $w_i=w$, $d_i=D$ for all $i$).
}

\begin{theorem}[Classification by homogeneity]\label{thm:classification}
\DIFadd{Let $T\subseteq\{p,w,d\}$.
}\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{If $p\in T$ or $w\in T$, MWHED restricted to $\mathcal C_T$
is solvable in $O(n\log n)$ time.
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{If $T\subseteq\{d\}$, MWHED restricted to $\mathcal C_T$ is
weakly NP-hard; in particular it is NP-hard for $T=\{d\}$.
}\end{enumerate}
\DIFadd{Thus, assuming $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$, an MWHED restriction is
polynomial exactly when the dispatch times or the weights are constant,
and constancy of the deadlines never helps.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{(a) If $p\in T$, apply Theorem~\ref{thm:equalp} with $m=1$. If $w\in T$,
all sites have the same weight $w$, so maximizing on-time weight means
maximizing the }\emph{\DIFadd{number}} \DIFadd{of on-time sites, which is the problem
$1\|\sum U_j$ solved in $O(n\log n)$ time by Moore's algorithm
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{moore1968}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. (b) $\mathcal C_{\{d\}}\supseteq$ the instances of
Theorem~\ref{thm:hardness}, so it is NP-hard, and it is }\emph{\DIFadd{weakly}}
\DIFadd{NP-hard in the usual sense (NP-hard, yet solvable in pseudo-polynomial
time by Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}); $\mathcal C_\emptyset$ contains
$\mathcal C_{\{d\}}$.
}\end{proof}

\DIFadd{The classification is an elementary corollary of
Theorems~\ref{thm:hardness} and~\ref{thm:equalp} and of Moore's
algorithm; its value is interpretive. It is also not the natural
tractability boundary in the scheduling literature, where
Moore--Hodgson's rule is known to extend to agreeable (oppositely
ordered) weights and dispatch times \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{lawler1976}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, a class
containing both constant $p$ and constant $w$. Hardness
needs }\emph{\DIFadd{two independent}} \DIFadd{sources of heterogeneity, in the dispatch
time }\emph{\DIFadd{and}} \DIFadd{in the criticality, which compete for the shared time
budget; heterogeneity in the hazard deadlines, the part of the instance
produced by the hazard model, plays no role. In network terms: if every
site is equally far from the depot, or every site is equally critical,
the dispatch problem is easy however the hazard spreads. Under the
arrival reading the same holds with ``constant hazard-arrival time'' in
place of ``constant deadline'' (Proposition~\ref{prop:consth}).
}

\subsection{\DIFadd{Hazard geometry: when the round-trip premise is costly, and what a front does}}\label{sec:geometry}

\DIFadd{The model abstracts the geometry into the pairs $(p_i,d_i)$. This
subsection asks four questions about what that abstraction hides and
what it keeps: how much a crew gains by chaining sites instead of making
round trips (Section~\ref{sec:model} claims only a one-sided bound); what
the chained problem looks like on the simplest network, a line; which
hardness survives when the deadlines are produced by an actual hazard
front, in particular a front that starts at the depot; and how well the
chained route can be approximated for such a front. All results are
for the arrival reading of Remark~\ref{rem:reading}. They are checked by
independent brute-force code (Section~\ref{sec:verify}) and their proofs
are in this subsection and Appendix~\ref{app:geometry}.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Setting.}} \DIFadd{Distances are travel times (the crew moves at unit
speed) in a metric $\delta$ on the depot $0$ and the sites; $a_i:=\delta(0,i)$;
the hazard reaches site $i$ at time $h_i$, and $F:=\{i:a_i\le h_i\}$ is the
set of sites that can be reached in time at all (a site outside $F$ is
never protected in either model, by the triangle inequality). In the
round-trip (spoke) model $p_i=2a_i$ and $d_i=h_i+a_i$, and the crew
arrives at the $j$-th site of an order $(i_1,\dots,i_k)$ at
$A_j=2\sum_{l<j}a_{i_l}+a_{i_j}$; if it travels directly between
consecutive sites it arrives at $B_j=a_{i_1}+\sum_{l<j}\delta(i_l,i_{l+1})$.
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$ is the optimum of the MWHED instance, and
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}$ the optimum over ordered subsets for the chained
route; Proposition~\ref{prop:spoke} gives
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}\le W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}$. $W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}(c)$
is the spoke optimum for a crew $c$ times faster. For $n\ge2$ let
$\kappa:=\min_{i\ne j}\delta(i,j)/(a_i+a_j)\in(0,1]$ (the }\emph{\DIFadd{clustering ratio}}\DIFadd{, positive because the sites are at distinct locations; small for clustered sites) and $\Gamma:=\max_{i\in F}a_i/\min_{i\in F}a_i$
(the }\emph{\DIFadd{aspect ratio}}\DIFadd{).
}

\begin{theorem}[The spoke--chain gap]\label{thm:gap}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{$W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}\le W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}\le\sum_{i\in F}w_i\le|F|\,W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$; in particular
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}\le n\,W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{The factor $n$ is attained: for every $n\ge2$ there are metric
instances with unit weights, $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}=n$ and
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}=1$, among them (i) }\emph{\DIFadd{clusters}}\DIFadd{, with all
$a_i=R$ and $\delta(i,j)=\varepsilon<2R/(n-1)$, and (ii) a }\emph{\DIFadd{ray}}\DIFadd{, with sites at
distances $r^j$ }\DIFaddend (\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{$p_i$ uniform on $\{1,\dots,20\}$,
$w_i$ uniform on
$\{1,\dots,100\}$, $d_i$ uniform on $\{1,\dots,\sum_i p_i\}$) }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$r>1$) on a half-line, which is a hazard front that
starts at the depot and moves at exactly the crew's speed.
}\item[\DIFadd{(c)}] \DIFadd{$W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}=W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$ for all
hazard times and weights if and only if $\delta(i,j)=a_i+a_j$ for all
$i\ne j$ (the depot lies on every shortest path between two sites: every
site is on its own branch of a tree rooted at the depot). If a pair violates it, there are
hazard times and weights with $W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}=1<2=W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}$.
}\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{(a) The first inequality is Proposition~\ref{prop:spoke}. Sites protected
by the chained route lie in $F$, which gives the second and third. The
heaviest site of $F$ served alone is spoke-feasible ($A_1=a_i\le h_i$), so
$w^F_{\max}\le W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$ and $\sum_Fw_i\le|F|w^F_{\max}$.
(b)(i) Chain order $1,\dots,n$ with $h_j=R+(j-1)\varepsilon$: $B_j=h_j$,
so all $n$ sites are protected. In the spoke model the later of two
protected sites arrives at time at least $2R+R=3R>R+(n-1)\varepsilon\ge h_j$, so
at most one site is protected. (ii) Chain outward with $h_j=r^j$:
$B_j=r^j=h_j$. In the spoke model the later of two protected sites
$i<j$ arrives at $2a_i+a_j>a_j=h_j$.
(c) If $\delta(i,j)=a_i+a_j$ for all pairs then
$B_j=a_{i_1}+\sum_{l<j}(a_{i_l}+a_{i_{l+1}})=A_j$ for every order. If
$\delta(i,j)<a_i+a_j$, set $h_i=a_i$, $h_j=a_i+\delta(i,j)$}\DIFaddend , \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$w_i=w_j=1$ }\DIFaddend and
\DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{compare four algorithms: the true optimum via Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}'s
exact dynamic program ; the FPTAS of }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$h_k=0$ for the other sites. The chain order $(i,j)$ gives $B_1=a_i\le h_i$,
$B_2=h_j$, so both are protected; in the spoke model order $(i,j)$ gives
$A_2=2a_i+a_j>h_j$ and order $(j,i)$ gives $A_2=2a_j+a_i>a_i=h_i$, so only
one site is protected. }\end{proof}

\DIFadd{Theorem~\ref{thm:gap}(c) sharpens the remark after
Proposition~\ref{prop:spoke}, and (b) shows that the gap is not an
artefact of clustered towns: it occurs for the most natural geometry of a
front that chases the crew along one road. The next result says which
parameters control the gap.
}

\begin{theorem}[Speed augmentation; no bound in $\kappa$ alone]\label{thm:speedaug}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{For every chained order and every $j\ge2$,
$A_j\le\bigl(B_j-(1-\kappa)a_{i_1}\bigr)/\kappa\le B_j/\kappa$, and $A_1=B_1$.
Consequently $W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}\le W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}\le W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}(1/\kappa)$,
a bracket whose ends are both computed by the dynamic program of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}.
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{The speed multiplier $1/\kappa$ of the bracket cannot be reduced in general: on the ray
of Theorem~\ref{thm:gap}(b)(ii), where $\kappa=\frac{r-1}{r+1}$, the
least speed multiplier at which the spoke crew protects all $n$ sites
equals $1+2\frac{1-r^{1-n}}{r-1}$, which tends to $1/\kappa$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(c)}] \DIFadd{There is no function $f$ with $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}\le f(\kappa)W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}$: for every
$\kappa_0\in(0,1)$ and $n$ the ray with $r=\frac{1+\kappa_0}{1-\kappa_0}$ has
$\kappa=\kappa_0$ and $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}=n$.
}\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{(a) For $j\ge2$, $\delta(i_l,i_{l+1})\ge\kappa(a_{i_l}+a_{i_{l+1}})$ gives
$B_j\ge a_{i_1}+\kappa\sum_{l<j}(a_{i_l}+a_{i_{l+1}})=(1-\kappa)a_{i_1}+\kappa A_j$.
With a crew $1/\kappa$ times faster the spoke arrival times are
$\kappa A_j\le B_j\le h_{i_j}$, so a chain-protected set is protected by the
same order. (b),(c) On the ray, the earliest-deadline order is the outward
order (Lemma~\ref{lem:edd}), and the spoke crew at speed $c$ reaches site
$k$ after sites $1,\dots,k-1$ in time $(2\sum_{j<k}r^j+r^k)/c\le r^k$ iff
$c\ge1+2(1-r^{1-k})/(r-1)$; (c) is Theorem~\ref{thm:gap}(b)(ii) with the
stated $r$. }\end{proof}

\DIFadd{(Part (b) concerns the outward order, which is the earliest-deadline order
on the ray; it does not show that the factor is needed for every instance
with the same $\kappa$.)
}

\begin{proposition}[A two-parameter bound]\label{prop:twopar}
\DIFadd{If $W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}>0$ and $n\ge2$,
}\[
\DIFadd{\frac{W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}}{W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}}\ \le\ \min\Bigl\{n,\ \Bigl\lceil\frac{3\Gamma-1}{2\kappa}\Bigr\rceil,\ \bigl(\lfloor\log_2\Gamma\rfloor+1\bigr)\Bigl\lceil\frac{5}{2\kappa}\Bigr\rceil\Bigr\}.
}\]
\DIFadd{For $\Gamma=1$ (all sites equally far from the depot) the bound is
$\lceil1/\kappa\rceil$, and it is attained up to rounding by the
}\emph{\DIFadd{uniform}} \DIFadd{family $a_i=1$, $\delta(i,j)=2\kappa$, $h_j=1+2\kappa(j-1)$,
whose ratio is $n/(\lfloor(n-1)\kappa\rfloor+1)$.
}\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{Take a chain-optimal route $(i_1,\dots,i_k)$ of protected sites with
extreme distances $a_{\min},a_{\max}\le\Gamma a_{\min}$, an integer
$m\ge(3\Gamma-1)/(2\kappa)$, and the residue classes
$J_r=\{j\equiv r\bmod m\}$ of the route positions. We show that each $J_r$
is protected in the spoke model in the induced order, so that
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}\ge\max_rw(J_r)\ge W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/m$. For
$j=r+(t-1)m$ with $t=1$ the spoke arrival is $a_{i_j}\le B_j\le h_{i_j}$.
For $t\ge2$ it is at most $2(t-1)a_{\max}+a_{i_j}$, while the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:speedaug}(a) gives
$B_j\ge(1-\kappa)a_{\min}+2\kappa(j-1)a_{\min}+\kappa a_{i_j}$ with
$j-1\ge(t-1)m$; the first is at most the second when
$2(t-1)\Gamma a_{\min}+(1-\kappa)(\Gamma-1)a_{\min}\le2\kappa(t-1)ma_{\min}$,
which holds because the left side is at most $(t-1)(3\Gamma-1)a_{\min}$ and
$2\kappa m\ge3\Gamma-1$. For the third bound split the route into
$\lfloor\log_2\Gamma\rfloor+1$ dyadic classes $a_i\in[2^qa_{\min},2^{q+1}a_{\min})$;
a sub-route of a chain-feasible route is chain-feasible (triangle
inequality), each class has aspect ratio below $2$, and the second bound
with $\Gamma<2$ gives $\lceil5/(2\kappa)\rceil$. The first term is
Theorem~\ref{thm:gap}(a). For the uniform family, the $t$-th protected
spoke site needs $2t-1\le h_j$, i.e.\ $j\ge1+(t-1)/\kappa$.
}\end{proof}

\DIFadd{On the Camp Fire instance $\kappa=0.078$ and $\Gamma=1.64$, so the bound
is vacuous there ($26>n=4$) and the explicit chained optimum of
Table~\ref{tab:campfire-route} is the right tool; the bracket and the
bound are informative for dispersed sites.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{A chained route on a line.}} \DIFadd{Put the depot at the origin of a
line, the sites at integer positions $x_i\ne0$, and let the crew move at
unit speed; site $i$ counts iff the crew is at $x_i$ at some time
$\le h_i$ (arrival reading, deadlines only). A walk that turns at extents
$e_1,e_2,\dots$ on alternating sides reaches the $k$-th turning point at
$2\sum_{j<k}e_j+e_k$, which is the spoke formula with $p_j=2e_j$; the
sites between turning points are collected on the way.
}

\begin{proposition}[One road]\label{prop:ray}
\DIFadd{If all sites lie on one side of the depot, $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}=\sum_{i:\,x_i\le h_i}w_i$.
}\end{proposition}

\DIFadd{(The crew walks straight out.) On a single road the chained problem is
therefore trivial, while the round-trip surrogate is the NP-hard MWHED;
with a front that starts at the depot and moves slower than the crew
($h_i=a_i/v$, $v\le1$) the chained route protects }\emph{\DIFadd{every}} \DIFadd{site and the
surrogate does not (Theorem~\ref{thm:gap}(b)(ii) and
Theorem~\ref{thm:affine} below).
}

\begin{theorem}[The weighted line problem]\label{thm:linealg}
\DIFadd{Let the left sites be sorted by distance $\ell_1\le\dots$ and the right
sites by distance $r_1\le\dots$.
}\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{The set of sites visited at any time is a prefix of each side, a walk can be taken to turn only at the outermost newly covered site, and
a dynamic program over the states (left prefix, right prefix, side) with the minimum time for each protected weight $v$
solves the problem in $O(n^2W)$ time, $W=\sum_iw_i$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{With equal weights the value is the number of protected sites and the program runs in $O(n^3)$ time.
}\item[\DIFadd{(c)}] \DIFadd{Scaling the weights as in }\DIFaddend Theorem~\ref{thm:fptas} \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{at
$\epsilon \in \{0.1,0.2\}$, implemented exactly as
in the proof (the
value-indexed dual DP, not a shortcut)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{gives an FPTAS in $O(n^4/\epsilon)$ time.
}\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[The weighted line problem is NP-hard]\label{thm:linehard}
\DIFadd{Weighted chained routing on a line with deadlines is NP-hard (weakly, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:linealg}(a)), even when the deadlines are those of a front
that starts at the depot at speed $2/3$ of the crew's, with a dispatch
delay. By Theorem~\ref{thm:linealg}(b) the hardness comes from the
weights alone.
}\end{theorem}

\emph{\DIFadd{Proof sketch of Theorem~\ref{thm:linealg}.}} \DIFadd{Every walk can be replaced
by one that reverses direction only at the outermost not yet covered site
of the side it is leaving, without delaying any first visit (shortcutting
and the triangle inequality); then the covered set is a pair of prefixes
and the state $(i,j,\text{side})$ plus the protected weight $v$ determines
the earliest time at which it can be reached, with $O(1)$ transitions.
Equal weights make $v\le n$. Theorem~\ref{thm:linealg} is a selective
version of the dynamic program of \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{tsitsiklis1992} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
for deadlines on a
line, which minimises the time to visit all sites; we do not claim it as
new and we give only this sketch. The hardness is proved in
Appendix~\ref{app:geometry}. We did not find a source that states the
complexity of the weighted selective problem; for the related line
problems with release times and for deadline-TSP see
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citet{psaraftis1990,afrati1986,baryehuda2005,farbstein2019}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
.
}

\paragraph{\DIFadd{Hazard fronts and affine deadlines.}} \DIFadd{Suppose the depot lies on
a shortest path from the hazard origin $H$ to every site (for instance
$H$ is the depot, or all sites lie beyond the depot on a line through
$H$), at distance $D\ge0$ from $H$, and the front moves at speed $v$. Then
$h_i=(D+a_i)/v$, so with $p_i=2a_i$ the deadlines are affine in the
dispatch times: $d_i=\beta+\alpha p_i$ with $\beta=D/v$ and
$\alpha=\tfrac12(1+\tfrac1v)$ under the arrival reading, $\alpha=\tfrac1{2v}$ under
the return reading. A front that starts at the depot has $\beta=0$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:consth} is the case $\alpha=\tfrac12$. For
$d_i=\beta+\alpha p_i$ and a set $S$ sorted by $p$ with prefix sums $T_k$,
feasibility is $T_k\le\beta+\alpha p_k$ for all $k$.
}

\begin{proposition}[Feasibility under affine deadlines]\label{prop:affine}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\DIFadd{(a)}] \DIFadd{If $0<\alpha\le1$, a set $S$ is feasible iff
$\sum_{i\in S}p_i-\alpha\max_{i\in S}p_i\le\beta$.
}\item[\DIFadd{(b)}] \DIFadd{If $\alpha>1$ and $\beta=0$, put $\lambda=\alpha$ and $\mu=\alpha-1$:
a set is feasible iff $T_{k-1}\le\mu p_k$ for all $k$, equivalently $T_k\ge(1+1/\mu)T_{k-1}$; hence every feasible set has
at most $1+\ln(\lambda R)/\ln(1+1/\mu)\le1+\lambda\ln(\lambda R)$ elements, where $R=p_{\max}/p_{\min}$.
}\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{(a) With $u_k:=T_k-\alpha p_k$, $u_{k+1}-u_k=\alpha p_k+(1-\alpha)p_{k+1}>0$,
so only the last constraint binds and $u_{|S|}=\sum_Sp-\alpha\max_Sp$.
(b) $T_k\le\alpha p_k$ is $T_{k-1}\le\mu p_k$, i.e.\
$T_k=T_{k-1}+p_k\ge(1+1/\mu)T_{k-1}$. Then $T_m\ge(1+1/\mu)^{m-1}p_{\min}$
and $T_m\le\alpha p_m\le\lambda p_{\max}$, and
$\ln(1+1/\mu)\ge1/\lambda$. }\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Classification of affine deadlines]\label{thm:affine}
\DIFadd{For a fixed rational $\alpha>0$ and $d_i=\beta+\alpha p_i$:
}\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{0.58\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
$\alpha$ & $\beta$ & complexity \\
\midrule
$<1$ & $\le0$ & trivial ($W^\ast=0$) \\
$<1$ & fixed constant & polynomial ($p_i\le\beta/(1-\alpha)$, so $P$ is polynomial) \\
$<1$ & part of the input & NP-hard, even with $w_i=p_i$ \\
$=1$ & $0$ & trivial ($W^\ast=w_{\max}$) \\
$=1$ & fixed constant & $O(n^2\beta)$ (guess the largest $p$, then a knapsack of capacity $\beta$) \\
$=1$ & part of the input & NP-hard, even with $w_i=p_i$ \\
$>1$ & $0$ (hence also $\beta$ in the input) & NP-hard (arbitrary weights) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\DIFadd{All hardness is in the ordinary (weak) sense and strong NP-hardness is
excluded unless $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$, by Theorem~\ref{thm:dp}.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{The trivial and polynomial rows follow from Proposition~\ref{prop:affine}
and Assumption~\ref{as:feasible}. For $\alpha\le1$ and $\beta$ in the
input, write $\alpha=u/q$ and reduce from }\textsc{\DIFadd{Partition}}
\DIFadd{$a_1,\dots,a_n$ with $A=\sum a_i$ even: $p_i=2qa_i$, $w_i=p_i$ for $i\ge1$,
$p_0=2qA+q$, $w_0=p_0$, $\beta=qA+(q-u)(2A+1)$, so $d_i=\beta+2ua_i$ and
$d_0=\beta+u(2A+1)$ are integers and $p_i\le d_i$; the target is
$T=p_0+qA$. Site $0$ has the largest $p$. If $0\in S$, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:affine}(a) $S$ is feasible iff
$\sum_{S\setminus0}p\le\beta-(1-\alpha)p_0=qA$, i.e.\ $\sum_{S\setminus0}a_i\le A/2$,
and then $w(S)\le T$ with equality iff the subset sums to $A/2$. If
$0\notin S$ then $w(S)\le2qA<T$. The case $\alpha>1$ is proved in
Appendix~\ref{app:geometry}. }\end{proof}

\DIFadd{So a hazard front never makes dispatch easy: even a front that starts at
the depot and moves slower than the crew ($v<1$, $\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(1+\frac{1}v)>1$,
$\beta=0$) gives an NP-hard MWHED. The hardness instances use sites at
exponentially spread distances; with the factor $\lambda$ in the input,
a thin ring suffices.
}

\begin{theorem}[A thin ring around the depot]\label{thm:annulus}
\DIFadd{MWHED with $d_i=\lambda p_i$ and $w_i=p_i$ is NP-hard when $\lambda$ is
part of the input, even if all dispatch times lie in an interval
$[K,K(1+O(1/n))]$ (all sites on a thin ring around a depot that is the
origin of the front), with $\lambda=n+\frac{1}{2}$.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{Let $x_1,\dots,x_n,t,k$ be positive integers, $X=\max x_i$,
$K=2kX+t+1$, $\mu=k-\frac{1}{2}$, $\lambda=k+\frac{1}{2}$, $H=(kK+t)/\mu$. The
sites (all with $d=\lambda p$, $w=p$) are }\emph{\DIFadd{real}} \DIFadd{sites with
$p_i=K+x_i$, $k$ }\emph{\DIFadd{dummy}} \DIFadd{sites with $p=K$, and one }\emph{\DIFadd{dominant}}
\DIFadd{site $D$ with $p_D=H$. Since $K>2(k-1)X$, any $k$ band sites are mutually
feasible; since $K\ge2kX$, $H>K+X$, so $D$ is the largest site. The last
site of a feasible $S$ completes at $P(S)\le\lambda p_{\max}(S)$ and
$W(S)=P(S)$; without $D$ this is $<\lambda H$, and with $D$ the
constraint at $D$ is $P(S\setminus D)\le\mu H=kK+t$, so
$W(S)\le\lambda H$, with equality iff $P(S\setminus D)=kK+t$. If
$T\subseteq[n]$ with $|T|\le k$ and $x(T)=t$, adding $k-|T|$ dummies and
$D$ attains $\lambda H$. Conversely, if $W(S)\ge\lambda H$ then $S\setminus D$
consists of $m$ band sites with $mK+x(T)=kK+t$}\DIFaddend ; \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{a weighted Moore--Hodgson-style
greedy repair heuristic (dispatch in earliest-deadline order,
}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$m\ge k+1$ would give
$(k+1)K>kK+t$ as $K>t$, and $m\le k-1$ would give
$x(T)\le(k-1)X<K$ and a total below $kK$, so $m=k$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{whenever the running schedule becomes infeasible, repeatedly drop the
scheduled site }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$x(T)=t$. For
}\textsc{\DIFadd{Partition}} \DIFadd{take $k=n$, $x=a$ and $t=A/2$. After multiplying all
sizes by $2(2k-1)$ every $p_i$, $d_i$ is an integer. }\end{proof}


\paragraph{\DIFadd{Approximating the chained route for a front.}} \DIFadd{The chained
problem with arbitrary deadlines is the maximum-weight version of
deadline-TSP, for which the best polynomial-time guarantee known is
$O(\log n)$ \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{bansal2004}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, and a constant factor is known only in
quasi-polynomial time \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{friggstad2021}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
. For the deadlines that a front
induces we can do better, with a guarantee that does not depend on $n$,
on the aspect ratio $\Gamma$, on $\kappa$ or on the weights. Let
$h_i=\beta+\lambda a_i$ }\DIFaddend with \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\lambda=1+\rho>1$ (a front that moves at
speed $1/\lambda<1$ of the crew's; the other cases are discussed after the
theorem) and $\beta\ge0$. For a route $(i_1,\dots,i_k)$ call
$e_j:=B_j-a_{i_j}\ge0$ the }\emph{\DIFadd{excess}} \DIFadd{of }\DIFaddend the \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{smallest weight-to-time ratio until feasible again---a natural, always-feasible heuristic generalizing
\mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citeauthor{moore1968}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
's unweighted rule}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$j$-th site. Put
$c(u,s):=\delta(u,s)+a_u-a_s$, which is non-negative by the triangle
inequality ($a_0:=0$). Then $e_1=0$ and $e_{j+1}=e_j+c(i_j,i_{j+1})$, so
}\emph{\DIFadd{the excess never decreases along a route}}\DIFadd{, and
}\begin{equation}\DIFadd{\label{eq:excess}
\text{site } i_j \text{ is protected}\iff e_j\le\beta+\rho\,a_{i_j}.
}\end{equation}
\DIFadd{Deleting sites from a route does not increase any remaining arrival
time (triangle inequality), hence no excess (shortcutting). If a block of
consecutive sites $i_r,\dots,i_s$ is started afresh from the depot, its excess
at $i_j$ becomes $e_j-e_r$ (restarting). The route in which every
site is protected needs, by~\eqref{eq:excess}, an excess budget that grows
linearly with the distance from the depot: this is what a front gives, and
what a general deadline does not.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Oracle.}} \DIFadd{We use a point-to-point orienteering algorithm as a black
box: given the metric, non-negative node prizes, the depot as source, a site
$t$ as sink and a length $L$, it returns a walk from the depot to $t$
of length at most $L$ whose collected prize is at least $1/\alpha$ of the
best such walk. Chekuri, Korula and P\'al \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{chekuri2012} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
give
$\alpha=2+\varepsilon$ in time $n^{O(1/\varepsilon^2)}$ for the
unweighted version, and Bansal et al.\ \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{bansal2004} }\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
give $\alpha=3$ in
strictly polynomial time; node prizes are reduced to the unweighted case by
discarding sites lighter than $\varepsilon/n$ times the heaviest one (in each
call the heaviest site alone is a feasible walk, so this loses at most an
$\varepsilon$-fraction) and replicating the rest at distance zero in
proportion to their rounded weights. We have not re-derived these results.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{Algorithm.}} \DIFadd{Fix $\theta\in(0,1)$, put $P:=\lceil2/\theta\rceil$ and let
$m$ be the least integer with $2\theta+4/\rho\le(1-\theta)(2^m-1)$. Let
$a_0:=\beta/\rho$ if $\beta>0$ and $a_0:=1$ if $\beta=0$. }\emph{\DIFadd{Class}} \DIFadd{$k$
consists of the sites with $a_i\in[a_02^k,a_02^{k+1})$; if $\beta>0$ the sites
with $a_i<a_0$ form class $-1$. Write $\alpha_k:=a_02^k$ ($\alpha_{-1}:=0$),
$f_k:=\beta+\rho\alpha_k$ (so that $f_k\le\beta+\rho a_i<2f_k$ for every
site $i$ of class $k$), $\Phi_k:=\theta f_k$ and $U_k:=a_02^{k+1}$ ($U_{-1}:=a_0$).
}\begin{enumerate}
\item \DIFadd{For every non-empty class $k$ and every site $t$ in it call the oracle
with the prizes $w_i$ on the sites of class $k$ (zero elsewhere), sink $t$ and
length $a_t+\Phi_k$. Let $R_k$ be the best of the resulting routes of
class-$k$ sites in order of first visit, with weight $V_k$.
}\item \DIFadd{Choose a set $K$ of classes whose indices differ pairwise by at
least $m$ so as to maximise $\sum_{k\in K}V_k$ (a dynamic program on the line).
}\item \DIFadd{Output the concatenation of the routes $R_k$}\DIFaddend , \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$k\in K$, in
increasing $k$.
}\end{enumerate}
\DIFadd{This is $n$ oracle calls and a linear-time dynamic program.
}

\begin{theorem}[A constant-factor approximation for a front]\label{thm:frontapprox}
\DIFadd{For $\lambda>1$ and $\beta\ge0$ the route returned protects every site it
contains, and its weight is at least $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/(\alpha Pm)$. With
$\theta=\tfrac12$ the factor is $4\alpha\lceil\log_2(3+8/\rho)\rceil$, which
is $16\alpha$ }\DIFaddend for \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{which we claim no
approximation guarantee ); }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\lambda=2$, $12\alpha$ for $\lambda=3$ }\DIFaddend and \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{the naive baseline of Proposition~\ref{prop:naive} (dispatch in earliest-deadline order, simply keep whichever sites happen to still be on time }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$8\alpha$ for every
$\lambda\ge9$. The guarantee depends only on $\lambda$: it is independent of
$n$, of $\Gamma$, of $\kappa$ and of the weights, and for fixed $\lambda$ it is a
constant.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{Call a route of class-$k$ sites }\emph{\DIFadd{$\Phi$-bounded}} \DIFadd{if all its excesses are
at most $\Phi$ (by monotonicity it suffices that the last one is), and let
$V(\Phi)$ be the largest weight of such a route.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{(A) The oracle step.}} \DIFadd{Let $W$ be the walk returned for the sink $t$, of length
$L\le a_t+\Phi_k$, and let $j$ be a class-$k$ site first visited at time
$\tau_j$ in $W$. The arrival time $B_j$ of the route $R(W)$ of first visits is at
most $\tau_j$ (shortcutting), and the rest of $W$ leads from $j$ to $t$, so
$\tau_j+\delta(j,t)\le L$ with $\delta(j,t)\ge a_t-a_j$. Hence
$e_j\le\tau_j-a_j\le L-(a_t-a_j)-a_j\le\Phi_k$: every candidate route is
$\Phi_k$-bounded. Conversely, an optimal $\Phi_k$-bounded route with last site
$t^\ast$ is itself a walk from the depot to $t^\ast$ of length $B_{t^\ast}=a_{t^\ast}+e_{t^\ast}\le a_{t^\ast}+\Phi_k$,
so the call with sink $t^\ast$ returns weight at least $V(\Phi_k)/\alpha$. Thus
$V_k\ge V(\Phi_k)/\alpha$.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{(B) Splitting.}} \DIFadd{Let $Q$ be a route of class-$k$ sites in which every site
is protected. By~\eqref{eq:excess} and $f_k\le\beta+\rho a<2f_k$, all its excesses lie in
$[0,2f_k)=[0,(2/\theta)\Phi_k)$. Cut $Q$ greedily: a new piece starts at the first
site whose excess exceeds the excess at the start of the current piece by
more than $\Phi_k$. Then at most $P$ pieces arise
(the $r$-th piece starts at excess $>(r-1)\Phi_k$), and by restarting each is a
$\Phi_k$-bounded route, so $w(Q)\le P\,V(\Phi_k)$.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{(C) Feasibility of the concatenation.}} \DIFadd{Let $k_1<\dots<k_s$ be the classes of $K$ (gaps at least $m$),
$R_{k_i}$ the routes with first and last sites $s_i,u_i$, and let $e(\cdot)$ denote
excesses in the concatenated route. By restarting and by (A),
$e(u_i)\le e(s_i)+\Phi_{k_i}$, and $e(s_{i+1})=e(u_i)+c(u_i,s_{i+1})$ with
$c(u,s)\le2a_u<2U_{k_i}$ (triangle inequality). With $e(s_1)=0$ this gives
$e(s_i)\le\sum_{l<i}g_{k_l}$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{with no repair at all). Table~\ref{tab:illustration} reports the ratio of each
method's on-time weight to the true optimum, averaged over the 20
instances at each size. Algorithm~\ref{alg:repair} states the greedy
repair heuristic precisely; feasibility of
its output is guaranteed by the same inductive argument used for the unweighted Moore--Hodgson
algorithm (each repair step can only shorten, never lengthen, the
completion time of every remaining scheduled site
)}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$g_k:=\Phi_k+2U_k$. For $\beta>0$ one checks
$g_k\le\beta2^k(2\theta+4/\rho)$ for all $k\ge-1$ (using $a_0=\beta/\rho$ and
$1+2^k\le2^{k+1}$); for $\beta=0$}\DIFaddend , \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{but---unlike
Algorithms~\ref{alg:exact}--\ref{alg:matroid}---we prove no
approximation ratio for it; it }\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$g_k\le\rho2^k(2\theta+4/\rho)$.
As $k_l\le k_i-m(i-l)$,
}\[
\DIFadd{e(s_i)\le\beta2^{k_i}(2\theta+4/\rho)\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-mj}
=\frac{\beta2^{k_i}(2\theta+4/\rho)}{2^m-1}\le(1-\theta)\beta2^{k_i}\le(1-\theta)f_{k_i}
}\]
\DIFadd{by the choice of $m$ (for $\beta=0$ replace $\beta$ by $\rho$ and $f_{k_i}=\rho2^{k_i}$).
Hence every site $j$ of $R_{k_i}$ has $e(j)\le(1-\theta)f_{k_i}+\Phi_{k_i}=f_{k_i}\le\beta+\rho a_j$,
i.e.\ it is protected by~\eqref{eq:excess}. The weight of the output is $\sum_{k\in K}V_k$.
}

\emph{\DIFadd{(D) Comparison with the optimum.}} \DIFadd{Let $Q^\ast$ be an optimal route; by shortcutting we may
assume that all its sites are protected. Its restriction to class $k$ is a route
of protected class-$k$ sites (shortcutting), of weight $\mathrm{st}_k$ say, and by (B) and (A)
$\mathrm{st}_k\le PV(\Phi_k)\le\alpha PV_k$. The $\mathrm{st}_k$ add up to $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}$,
so for some residue $r$ modulo $m$ the classes $k\equiv r$ carry at least
$W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/m$. This family has gaps at least $m$, so it is admissible in
step~2, and the output has weight at least $\sum_{k\equiv r}V_k\ge W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/(\alpha Pm)$.
}\end{proof}

\emph{\DIFadd{Remarks.}} \DIFadd{(i) The factor grows like $\log_2(1/(\lambda-1))$ as
$\lambda\downarrow1$, and this growth is real for the analysis (decoupling the
classes loses $\Theta(\log(1/\rho))$ on stars with legs of lengths $2^k$);
whether an absolute constant exists is open to us. (ii) For $\lambda=1$ and
$\beta=0$ a route is protected iff all its excesses vanish, i.e.\ iff
every leg satisfies $a_u+\delta(u,s)=a_s$; this is a longest-path problem in a
directed acyclic graph, solved exactly. For $\lambda=1$ and $\beta>0$ a route
is protected iff its last excess is at most $\beta$, which is one point-to-point
orienteering instance per last site, with ratio $\alpha$. For $\lambda<1$ and $\beta=0$ no site
can be reached in time; $\lambda<1$ with $\beta>0$ is not covered. (iii) The theorem is
an application of an orienteering algorithm; the new ingredient is the
excess~\eqref{eq:excess}, which turns the front's deadlines into a budget that
is proportional to the distance and so lets dyadic distance classes be
decoupled. It does not say anything about general deadlines, for which
a polynomial-time constant factor remains open. (iv) We ran the algorithm with an exact oracle on $450$ random instances
with $n\le9$ and compared it with the exact chained optimum
(Section~\ref{sec:verify}); the route was always protected and the weight
always above the guaranteed fraction (the worst observed ratio to the
optimum was $0.31$). The lemmas of the proof were also checked with a
deliberately weak oracle. The bound is not tight.
}

\DIFadd{The round-trip surrogate cannot deliver such a guarantee at any fixed speed.
}

\begin{proposition}[No surrogate bound for a front]\label{prop:nosurrogate}
\DIFadd{Let the sites lie at the positions $1,\dots,n$ of a half-line, with unit
weights and $h_i=\lambda a_i$. Then $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}=n$, while for every
speed multiplier $c$ with $c\lambda>1$ and $q:=(c\lambda-1)/2$,
}\[
\DIFadd{W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}(c)\ \le\ 1+\frac{\ln\bigl(n(n+1)/2\bigr)}{\ln(1+1/q)} ,
}\]
\DIFadd{and $W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}(c)\le1$ if $c\lambda\le1$. So for fixed $\lambda$ and $c$ the
ratio $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}/W^\ast_{\mathrm{spoke}}(c)$ }\DIFaddend is \DIFdelbegin \DIFdel{included as
a realistic, easy-to-implement point of comparison, not as a fifth theorem}\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \DIFadd{$\Omega(n/\log n)$}\DIFaddend .
\DIFaddbegin \end{proposition}
\DIFaddend 

\DIFdelbegin %DIFDELCMD < \begin{algorithm}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
%DIFDELCMD < \caption{%
{%DIFAUXCMD
\DIFdel{Weighted greedy repair (heuristic; no proven guarantee)}}%DIFAUXCMD
%DIFDELCMD < \label{alg:repair}
%DIFDELCMD < \begin{algorithmic}[1]
%DIFDELCMD < \Require sites $1,\dots,n$ with $d_1\le\dots\le d_n$, dispatch times $p_i$, weights $w_i$
%DIFDELCMD < \Ensure a feasible on-time subset $\mathit{kept}$ (not necessarily optimal)
%DIFDELCMD < \State $\mathit{kept} \gets \emptyset$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets 0$
%DIFDELCMD < \For{$i = 1,\dots,n$}
%DIFDELCMD <   \State $\mathit{kept} \gets \mathit{kept} \cup \{i\}$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets \mathit{total} + p_i$
%DIFDELCMD <   \While{$\mathit{total} > d_i$ \textbf{and} $\mathit{kept} \ne \emptyset$}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $j \gets \arg\min_{j' \in \mathit{kept}} w_{j'}/p_{j'}$ \Comment{smallest weight-to-time ratio}
%DIFDELCMD <     \State $\mathit{kept} \gets \mathit{kept} \setminus \{j\}$; \ $\mathit{total} \gets \mathit{total} - p_j$
%DIFDELCMD <   \EndWhile
%DIFDELCMD < \EndFor
%DIFDELCMD < \State \Return $\mathit{kept}$, dispatched in increasing-deadline order
%DIFDELCMD < \end{algorithmic}
%DIFDELCMD < \end{algorithm}
%DIFDELCMD < %%%
\DIFdelend \DIFaddbegin \begin{proof}
\DIFadd{The chained route walks out and arrives at the $j$-th site at time $j\le\lambda j$.
A set of positions $p_1<\dots<p_k$ is protected in the surrogate iff
$(2S_{j-1}+p_j)/c\le\lambda p_j$ for all $j$ (earliest-deadline order is the outward order),
where $S_j=p_1+\dots+p_j$, i.e.\ $S_{j-1}\le q(S_j-S_{j-1})$. For $j\ge2$ this
forces $q>0$ and $S_j\ge(1+1/q)S_{j-1}$, so $S_k\ge(1+1/q)^{k-1}$, while
$S_k\le n(n+1)/2$ because the $p_l$ are distinct integers in $[1,n]$.
}\end{proof}

\DIFadd{Thus Theorem~\ref{thm:speedaug}(a) cannot be strengthened to a bracket with a speed
multiplier that depends on $\lambda$ alone, and the algorithm above must
use the chained structure.
}

\DIFadd{How good can a guarantee for a front be? Not arbitrarily good, even when
all the weights are equal and all the sites are equally far from the
depot. Then every route has $B_j=R+L_j$, where $R$ is the common distance
and $L_j$ the length of the path through the first $j$ sites, so
$h_i=\beta+\lambda R$ turns the problem into path-orienteering with budget
$\beta+(\lambda-1)R$.
}

\begin{theorem}[Unit weights are strongly NP-hard]\label{thm:frontnp}
\DIFadd{For every fixed rational $\lambda>1$ the chained problem with unit weights,
$\beta=0$ and $h_i=\lambda a_i$ is strongly NP-hard, even if all sites are at
the same distance from the depot ($\Gamma=1$) and the metric is a graph
metric with inter-site distances in $\{1,2\}$. Consequently there is no
FPTAS and no pseudo-polynomial algorithm for it unless $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$.
}\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\DIFadd{Reduce from }\textsc{\DIFadd{Hamiltonian path}} \DIFadd{on a graph $G$ with $n\ge\lambda$
vertices. The sites are the vertices; $\delta(i,j)=1$ if $ij\in E(G)$ and $2$
otherwise; $a_i=R:=(n-1)/(\lambda-1)$ for all $i$; all weights are $1$ and
$h_i=\lambda R$. The triangle inequalities hold because the inter-site
distances are in $\{1,2\}$ and $2\le2R$ when $n\ge\lambda$. For a route of
$k$ sites, $B_j=R+L_j$ and site $i_j$ is protected iff $L_j\le(\lambda-1)R=n-1$.
A route of all $n$ sites has $L_n\ge n-1$ with equality iff every leg has
length~$1$, i.e.\ iff it is a Hamiltonian path of $G$. So $W^\ast_{\mathrm{chain}}=n$
iff $G$ has one. Multiplying all distances by the denominator of
$\lambda-1$ makes the data integer and polynomially bounded; hence the
problem is strongly NP-hard, and an FPTAS would distinguish
$W^\ast=n$ from $W^\ast\le n-1$.
}\end{proof}

\DIFadd{(On these instances the round-trip surrogate protects only
$\lfloor(\lambda+1)/2\rfloor$ sites.) The same instances with the
budget $n-1$ replaced by every possible length $T$ turn a
$\rho$-approximation of the protected count into a
$(1+2(1-1/\rho)n/(n-1))$-approximation of the $(1,2)$-travelling-salesman
path (complete the path greedily, at cost at most $2$ per missing site);
as the $(1,2)$-TSP is APX-hard \mbox{%DIFAUXCMD
\citep{papadimitriou1993}}\hskip0pt%DIFAUXCMD
, there is
no polynomial-time approximation scheme unless $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$. We
only sketch this, we have not checked the path version of the hardness
result separately, and the constant it excludes is very small. The achievable ratio therefore lies
between a number just above $1$ and $O(\log(1/(\lambda-1)))$ for fixed
$\alpha$.
}

%DIF >  ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\section{\DIFadd{Numerical study}}\label{sec:experiments}

\DIFadd{The study has four purposes: to check every algorithm against an
